LLM supporting:  Supporting sentence: The p53 protein functions as a transcription factor, regulating downstream genes involved in cell cycle arrest, DNA repair and programmed cell death. Loss of p53 function confers genomic instability, impaired apoptosis and diminished cell cycle restraint. Therefore, p53 mutations select for certain critical features of malignancy. Alteration of P53 is the most common mutation in human cancer. Roughly half of all human malignancies, including many urological cancers, exhibit p53 mutations. In bladder cancer p53 mutations have been associated with higher tumor grade and advanced stage, as well as progression of superficial disease to muscle invasion. Moreover, p53 nuclear over expression appears to be an independent predictor of disease progression and decreased survival after cystectomy.

This review summarizes the pathological roles of the NCX as a new target for NO-mediated cellular toxicity, based on our studies on NO-NCX-mediated glial toxicity.

The mechanisms leading to alpha-synuclein fragmentation and aggregation into extracellular amyloid are not known, although alpha-synuclein fragment and betaA4 aggregates are the result of abnormal cleavage of large precursors. On the other hand, several studies have shown that alpha-synuclein may adopt a fibrilar conformation and give rise to insoluble forms and high molecular weight aggregates in vitro. Similar complexes have also been observed in alpha-synucleinopathies. Although studies in vitro and in vivo have shown toxic effects of alpha-synuclein, the consequence of alpha-synuclein deposition on cell survival in alpha-synucleinopathies is not known.

LLM supporting:  Supporting sentence: During mitosis, the nuclear envelope breaks down and cytoplasmic proteins may enter what was formerly a privileged zone. To address the hypothesis that all APOBEC3 proteins have access to genomic DNA, we analyzed the localization of the APOBEC3 proteins during mitosis. We show that APOBEC3A, APOBEC3C and APOBEC3H are excluded from condensed chromosomes, but become cell-wide during telophase. However, APOBEC3B, APOBEC3D, APOBEC3F and APOBEC3G are excluded from chromatin throughout mitosis. Both structural motifs as well as size may be factors in regulating chromatin exclusion. Deaminase activity was not dependent on cell cycle phase. We also analyzed APOBEC3-induced cell cycle perturbations as a measure of each enzyme's capacity to inflict genomic DNA damage. AID, APOBEC3A and APOBEC3B altered the cell cycle profile, and, unexpectedly, APOBEC3D also caused changes. We conclude that several APOBEC3 family members have access to the nuclear compartment and can impede the cell cycle, most likely through DNA deamination and the ensuing DNA damage response. Such genomic damage may contribute to carcinogenesis, as demonstrated by AID in B cell cancers and, recently, APOBEC3B in breast cancers.

LLM supporting:  The measurement of bladder wall thickness (BWT) is a critical parameter in the assessment of benign prostatic hyperplasia (BPH) and other lower urinary tract conditions. Bladder wall thickness can be measured using various imaging modalities, with ultrasound being one of the most commonly used and non-invasive techniques. Specifically, transrectal ultrasound (TRUS) and transabdominal ultrasound (TAUS) are frequently employed to measure BWT. TRUS involves the use of a probe inserted into the rectum, providing a more direct and often more accurate view of the bladder and prostate. TAUS, on the other hand, uses a probe placed on the abdomen, which can also provide reliable measurements, especially when combined with other parameters such as bladder volume. Recent advancements in technology, including three-dimensional (3D) ultrasound and automated measurement software, have further improved the accuracy and repeatability of BWT measurements. These technologies not only enhance the precision of BWT but also contribute to the standardization of the measurement process, which is essential for clinical research and daily practice.

LLM supporting:  Supporting sentence: In a double-blind placebo-controlled prospective clinical trial we studied the efficacy and safety of the benzodiazepine antagonist, flumazenil. In 23 patients admitted to the Intensive Care Unit with coma due to overdose with benzodiazepines or other sedatives, flumazenil i.v. (up to 2 mg or placebo) was given. In 13 patients given flumazenil the Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) increased significantly from 4.9 to 7.8 (p less than 0.05). Six of these 13 patients, including mainly benzodiazepine mono-intoxications, needed only one series of injections (up to 1.0 mg flumazenil); the GCS increased thereby from 4.5 to 10.7 within a maximum of 5 min (p less than 0.01). In the remaining 7 patients, needing two series of injections of flumazenil (up to 2.0 mg), GCS did not rise significantly and coma was related to intoxications with nonbenzodiazepine sedatives, flunitrazepam and in one patient, encephalitis. In the 10 patients receiving placebo, the GCS did not change. A significant increase in the GCS from 5.5 to 10.8 (p less than 0.001) was, however, observed when flumazenil (up to 1.0 mg) was given after placebo. In patients with EEG monitoring the changes in waveform pattern paralleled the clinical response. Effects could be detected within 1-2 min after flumazenil injection and lasted up to 45 min. There were no adverse reactions or benzodiazepine withdrawal symptoms.

Accumulations of alpha-synuclein in all these disorders have in common a fibrilar configuration, but they differ in the binding of alpha-synuclein to distinct proteins with the exception of ubiquitin whose binding to alpha-synuclein is common to all alpha-synuclein inclusions. The mechanisms leading to alpha-synuclein fragmentation and aggregation into extracellular amyloid are not known, although alpha-synuclein fragment and betaA4 aggregates are the result of abnormal cleavage of large precursors. On the other hand, several studies have shown that alpha-synuclein may adopt a fibrilar conformation and give rise to insoluble forms and high molecular weight aggregates in vitro. Although studies in vitro and in vivo have shown toxic effects of alpha-synuclein, the consequence of alpha-synuclein deposition on cell survival in alpha-synucleinopathies is not known.

=== idx: 370 ===
Gold supporting: However, because some archaeal genomes contain multiple origins of replication, bias in gene dosage caused by delayed replication should be minimized and hence the substitution rate of genes should associate less with chromosome position.
LLM supporting:  Supporting sentence: In bacterial chromosomes, the position of a gene relative to the single origin of replication generally reflects its replication timing, how often it is expressed, and consequently, its rate of evolution. Ortholog families were grouped by their consensus position and designated by their proximity to one of the three origins (O1, O2, O3). Conserved orthologs were concentrated near the origins and most variation in genome content occurred distant from the origins. Linear regressions of both synonymous and nonsynonymous substitution rates on distance from replication origins were significantly positive, the rates being greatest in the region furthest from any of the origins and slowest among genes near the origins. Genes near O1 also evolved faster than those near O2 and O3, which suggest that this origin may fire later in the cell cycle. Increased evolutionary rates and gene dispensability are strongly associated with reduced gene expression caused in part by reduced gene dosage during the cell cycle. Therefore, in this genus of Archaea as well as in many Bacteria, evolutionary rates and variation in genome content associate with replication timing.

This condition can lead to the deposition of calcium in various tissues, including the kidneys (nephrocalcinosis), heart, and blood vessels, and can contribute to the development of arteriopathy. Additionally, there is an increased risk of developing cataracts, which may be related to the hypercalcemic state. In some cases, long QT interval syndrome, a condition characterized by prolonged heart muscle relaxation, can occur, which may be due to the hypercalcemic effect on cardiac function. Furthermore, individuals with Williams syndrome may experience early puberty, which can be managed with gonadotropin-releasing hormone (GnRH) agonists to suppress the premature activation of the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis. These hormonal and metabolic complications are significant aspects of the clinical presentation of Williams syndrome and require careful management.

=== idx: 577 ===
Gold supporting: Inheritance seems most likely to be autosomal recessive.
LLM supporting:  Supporting sentence: Wilson's disease is inherited in an autosomal recessive pattern. This means that an individual with Wilson's disease has two copies of the mutant gene, one from each parent. The parents of the affected individual are typically carriers, meaning they have one copy of the mutant gene and one copy of the normal gene. In most cases, the mutation is de novo, meaning it is not inherited from either parent but rather occurs as a new mutation in the affected individual. However, in some cases, the mutation can be inherited from one or both parents, leading to the affected individual having two copies of the mutant gene. The autosomal recessive pattern of inheritance is responsible for the wide range of clinical manifestations seen in Wilson's disease, as the severity and specific symptoms can vary depending on the specific genetic mutation and other factors such as environmental influences and modifier genes.

=== idx: 592 ===
Gold supporting: While SAMe is a methyl donor, MTA is an inhibitor of methylation.
LLM supporting:  S-adenosylmethionine (SAMe) is the primary methyl donor for histone methyltransferases. This modification plays a crucial role in gene regulation, particularly in the context of inflammation. In the study, it was shown that LPS-induced increases in the binding of trimethylated H3K4 to the TNFα and iNOS promoters were completely blocked by either SAMe or MTA pretreatment. This suggests that the inhibitory effect of SAMe on proinflammatory mediators is mediated through the modulation of histone methylation, with MTA being a key intermediate in this process. Additionally, S-adenosylhomocysteine (SAH), a byproduct of the methylation reaction, also inhibits the binding of H3K4 to these promoters, further supporting the role of the methionine cycle in regulating inflammatory gene expression.

On the other hand, several studies have shown that alpha-synuclein may adopt a fibrilar conformation and give rise to insoluble forms and high molecular weight aggregates in vitro. Similar complexes have also been observed in alpha-synucleinopathies. Although studies in vitro and in vivo have shown toxic effects of alpha-synuclein, the consequence of alpha-synuclein deposition on cell survival in alpha-synucleinopathies is not known.

LLM supporting:  Lubag disease, also known as X-linked dystonia-parkinsonism, is a rare genetic disorder characterized by the presence of both dystonia and parkinsonian features. Deep brain stimulation (DBS) has been shown to be an effective treatment for managing the motor symptoms of Lubag disease, and it is often performed under general anesthesia, which can be maintained with a combination of propofol, remifentanil, rocuronium bromide, and sevoflurane. During specific procedures, such as magnetic resonance imaging, additional anesthetic agents like midazolam, fentanyl, and rocuronium bromide may be used to ensure the patient remains appropriately sedated and still.

Gold supporting: Mutations in human cardiac calsequestrin (CASQ2), a high-capacity calcium-binding protein located in the sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR), have recently been linked to effort-induced ventricular arrhythmia and sudden death (catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia).

=== idx: 708 ===
Gold supporting: CT of the brain revealed characteristic features diagnostic of infantile type of cerebral hemiatrophy or Dyke-Davidoff-Masson syndrome.
LLM supporting:  The characteristic feature of Dyke-Davidoff-Masson syndrome (DDMS) is the presence of abnormal, non-keratinized epidermal cells in the brain. These cells are typically found in the subarachnoid space and can also infiltrate the cerebral parenchyma. This condition is associated with a range of clinical manifestations, including recurrent generalized seizures, spastic hemiplegia, microcephaly, and developmental delays in motor and speech domains, as seen in the 14-month-old male child. The syndrome is caused by mutations in the ABCB11 gene, which encodes a multidrug resistance protein involved in the transport of various substances, including retinoids, across the endoplasmic reticulum. The accumulation of these epidermal cells in the brain is thought to disrupt normal neural function, leading to the diverse neurological symptoms observed in patients with DDMS.

=== idx: 776 ===
Gold supporting: Pendred syndrome is a recessive inherited disorder that consists of developmental abnormalities of the cochlea, sensorineural hearing loss, and diffuse thyroid enlargement (goiter).
LLM supporting:  Pendred syndrome is not typically associated with specific hormone abnormalities. It is a genetic disorder characterized by a mutation in the PDS gene, which encodes a chloride-iodide transport protein. This mutation leads to an imbalance in the endolymphatic fluid, resulting in sensorineural hearing loss and, in some cases, goiter. The primary issue in Pendred syndrome is the accumulation of chloride ions in the endolymph, which can lead to the development of endolymphatic hydrops, a condition where there is an excessive accumulation of endolymph in the inner ear. This can cause damage to the hair cells and supporting structures in the cochlea, leading to the characteristic hearing loss. While the syndrome may be associated with iodine metabolism, as suggested by the presence of goiter in some patients, this is not a hormonal abnormality per se but rather a functional one. The mainstay of treatment for Pendred syndrome is supportive, focusing on managing symptoms and preventing further damage to the auditory system.

=== idx: 863 ===
Gold supporting: CaM kinase II activation was inhibited by KN-93 pretreatment (IC(50) approximately 1 microM).
LLM supporting:  Supporting sentence: The goals of this study were to determine: 1) to what extent CaM kinase II is activated by contractile stimuli in intact arterial smooth muscle, and 2) the effect of a CaM kinase II inhibitor (KN-93) on CaM kinase II activation, phosphorylation of myosin regulatory light chains (MLC(20)), and force. Both histamine (1 microM) and KCl depolarization activated CaM kinase II with a time course preceding maximal force development, and suprabasal CaM kinase II activation was sustained during tonic contractions. KN-93 inhibited histamine-induced tonic force maintenance, whereas early force development and MLC(20) phosphorylation responses during the entire time course were unaffected. Both force development and maintenance in response to KCl were inhibited by KN-93. Rapid increases in KCl-induced MLC(20) phosphorylation were also inhibited by KN-93, whereas steady-state MLC(20) phosphorylation responses were unaffected. In contrast, phorbol 12,13-dibutyrate (PDBu) did not activate CaM kinase II and PDBu-stimulated force development was unaffected by KN-93. Thus KN-93 appears to target a step(s) essential for force maintenance in response to physiological stimuli, suggesting a role for CaM kinase II in regulating tonic contractile responses in arterial smooth muscle. Pharmacological activation of protein kinase C bypasses the KN-93 sensitive step.

The mechanisms leading to alpha-synuclein fragmentation and aggregation into extracellular amyloid are not known, although alpha-synuclein fragment and betaA4 aggregates are the result of abnormal cleavage of large precursors. On the other hand, several studies have shown that alpha-synuclein may adopt a fibrilar conformation and give rise to insoluble forms and high molecular weight aggregates in vitro. Similar complexes have also been observed in alpha-synucleinopathies. Although studies in vitro and in vivo have shown toxic effects of alpha-synuclein, the consequence of alpha-synuclein deposition on cell survival in alpha-synucleinopathies is not known.

A specific measurement of direct FXa inhibitors is feasible with the use of a new test developed by the authors' group. The physicians must be aware of the possibility to measure the plasma concentration of FXa inhibitors in patients at high risk of bleeding and in several other special clinical situations.

=== idx: 963 ===
Gold supporting: To compare repeatability and agreement of conventional ultrasound bladder wall thickness (BWT) measurements with automatically obtained BWT measurements by the BVM 6500 device.
LLM supporting:  Bladder wall thickness (BWT) can be measured using various methods, including conventional ultrasound and the BVM 6500 device. Conventional ultrasound is a well-established method that involves the use of high-frequency sound waves to create images of the bladder, allowing for the measurement of the bladder wall's thickness. This technique is widely used and has been shown to be effective in clinical practice. On the other hand, the BVM 6500 device is a more recent addition to the field, which uses a different technology, likely involving a combination of electrical and mechanical principles, to measure BWT. However, it is important to note that the BVM 6500 device has limitations, as it is not able to correctly measure BWTs above 4 mm. Both methods have been found to be repeatable and agree with each other, with a mean difference in BWT values between the measurements of one technique ranging from -0.1 to +0.01 mm. The measurement variation between replicate measurements is smaller for conventional ultrasound, and the smallest for a 250 ml bladder filling. In conclusion, while the BVM 6500 device is a promising tool, conventional ultrasound remains a reliable and widely used method for measuring bladder wall thickness.

Gold supporting: The fly complex contains a catalytic SET domain subunit, E(Z), plus three noncatalytic subunits, SU(Z)12, ESC, and NURF-55.
LLM supporting:  Supporting sentence: Histone methyltransferases (HMTases) are characterized by the presence of a conserved SET (Suppression of Tetrads) domain. This domain is crucial for the enzymatic activity of HMTases, as it facilitates the transfer of methyl groups from S-adenosylmethionine (SAM) to specific lysine residues on histone proteins. The SET domain is found in various HMTases, including those in the Polycomb group (PcG) repressors, such as E(Z) in Drosophila melanogaster, which is a key component of the ESC-E(Z) complex. This complex is a multi-subunit histone methyltransferase that plays a critical role in silencing Hox genes and other developmental regulators. The SET domain's ability to catalyze methylation is enhanced by interactions with other subunits, such as ESC and SU(Z)12, which provide additional structural and functional support. In the context of the worm, the E(Z) homolog, MES-2, also contains a SET domain, and its HMTase activity is dependent on the presence of MES-6, an ESC homolog, and MES-3, a pioneer protein. Thus, the characteristic SET domain is a common feature among histone methyltransferases, enabling them to perform their essential roles in epigenetic regulation across different species.
